884. JOHN WATLING and ELIZA WATLING were again indicted for assaulting Alfred Edwin Hill, upon which MR. CULPEPER offered no evidence.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18830910-885]
885. CORNELIUS COOPER, JOHN TAYLOR (37), and THOMAS TAYLOR (33) , Unlawfully conspiring to cheat and defraud George Verrall and others of their property. Other counts for conspiring to steal from McCorquodale and Co. their property. Other counts for forging and uttering certain tickets, and for defrauding Thomas Hoodless of his property.
MR. GILL Prosecuted; MR. GEOGHEGAN appeared for Cooper, and MR. KEITH FRITH for the Taylors.
ALFRED BRUSHWOOD . I live at 48, City Road, and am a lithographic printer in the service of McCorquodale, printers, Thomas's Street, Borough—they have been employed for some time past to print race-course tickets—I know John Taylor—I have seen him and his brother together on several occasions—I met him one day some time ago, and he said "This is my brother"—John Taylor had been in the employment of the firm, and knew all about the printing of these racecourse tickets—I used to see him at home; I was sent there when he stopped away from work—after he was discharged I used to meet him at Goswell Road—I remember the order coming just before the August Bank Holiday, to print the tickets for the Bank Holiday meeting—I received the cardboard for them to be printed on—each card would contain 12 tickets—I printed 179 of the 180 cards, and kept one in the office for a copy; that left 178—172 of those I placed on the bench before Mr.
Lindsey—that left six cards of 12 tickets each, 72 tickets—about 7.30 on the Monday week before Bank Holiday, I met John Taylor at the bottom of Fore Street—he asked me if the Croydon tickets were in; I said "Yes"—he said "I dare say you will have to print them;" I said "Yes"—he said "Will you bring me out some?" I said "Yes"—he said "I will meet you on Wednesday evening at the same place"—I gave him the six cards in the street, and he asked me if I would go and have a glass of ale with him; I did so—he had left the employment; I think he was discharged at Whitsuntide—I know he had printed the race cards when he was in the employment—then they would not come into my hands at all—he had spoken to me at Whitsuntide about the race cards; he was at work then at the same time as I was on the premises—the cards I gave him on the Wednesday were of this description ( produced) when I gave them to him, but they had no number whatever on them—they were printed on both sides, and the number and "6 Aug., 1883" is on both sides—it is a card printed for this particular meeting, from a stone prepared for the particular card.
Cross-examined by MR. GEOGHEGAN. I have been a lithographic printer, I should think, for 14 years—I have never heard of any stuff that will erase printer's ink—I do not think you can take it out; I am not certain of it; I would not swear to it—I know nothing about type.
Cross-examined by MR. FRITH. I have been a little over two yean in McCorquodale's employment—they are good masters to me—Taylor had left some weeks when the interview first spoken of occurred—there is no one to corroborate me as to that interview—I did not know I was going to steal my master's goods—I should not have taken them if I had not been asked for them; I knew I was taking what was not mine; I did not know I was stealing—I knew I was taking cards without their belonging to me—I don't know that the firm's orders are not to part with these tickets to anybody—I knew I had no right to them; I should not have done it—I was charged at the police-court—I had to tell the truth; it made me so ill I was obliged to—Langrish had me down in the office and talked to me about having the cards—I did not know that by making these statements I should be let off—after I was discharged I expected to be called as a witness; I hoped to be let off for my wife and family.
Re-examined. I went to the police-court to be called as a witness; before I was called I saw Langrish; I did not tell him anything—I was charged on Thursday morning by the firm, by Mr. Langrish—before he charged me I had not told him a word.
JAMES LINDSEY . I am foreman of the lithographers at Messrs. McCorquodale's works—I received orders to print 2,000 enclosure tickets for the Croydon racecourse some little time before Bank Holiday—they were printed from a design prepared for the purpose on stone—after Brush-wood had printed them I took them from the bench and gave them to Stevens to cut up into tickets.
WILLIAM STEVENS . I am a cutter in the service of McCorquodale's—I cut the tickets and sent them to be numbered by Cole—when numbered from 1 to 2,000 they were returned to me; the same number were returned as I had sent—there was a surplus, which I put into a drawer and afterwards destroyed.
Cross-examined by MR. GEOGHEGAN. I cannot say if there is any stuff that will erase printing ink.
ALFRED COLE . I am a numerical printer in the service of McCorquodale's—I received the tickets to number, and handed them over to Mary White to do—I did not see them after they were numbered—I know the numbers—several tickets were shown me at the police-court, and amongst them were tickets that had not been numbered by me; they were not ours at all, duplicates—Langrish showed me some type and some printing ink in a paper—to the best of my belief I should say that type would produce the numbers on the forged tickets—the lines on these betting cards ( produced) have been erased and fresh ones stamped in on both sides—the first of these cards ( produced) is all right; it has got the numbers on; the second one has had the lines erased; I can read the remainder of the numbers—on the third card fresh lines have been put on—the red and black ink are both printing ink—I stamped these numbers with the type and ink.
Cross-examined by MR. GEOGHEGAN. I have seen the bottle found on Taylor; this is it ( produced)—I have had twelve or fourteen years' experience, and know no chemical that will erase printing ink completely—I have not tried this stuff—the type on Hoodless's tickets is not of a very common fount—it is not very uncommon for tickets; the figures are cut nicer—I have seen similar type before.
Re-examined. I never saw any type cut on both sides before.
MARY ELLEN WHITE . I am employed at McCorquodale's to number—I numbered the tickets for the August Bank Holiday meeting—some of the tickets shown me bear our numbers and some do not—I have looked through them.
FREDERICK CATHCART . I live at Ferndale Road, Clapham, and am manager to the Croydon Racecourse Committee—Mr. Frederick Verrall is clerk of the course—we issue tickets for entrance to the large enclosure, at 2 s.—before the Bank Holiday meeting we had 2,000 tickets printed for that 2 s. enclosure, numbered 1 to 2,000 consecutively, by McCorquodale's—the practice is, a person buys a ticket at the box and gives it up at the entrance as he goes in—anybody coming out would get a pass, which Would be one of the same tickets as sold at the box—neither of the prisoners are connected in any way with the committee—after the day's racing on Bank Holiday the tickets taken on that day were brought to me by the checktakers—I handed them to Langrish, who found among them 48 forged tickets—I am a wood engraver; I can tell the difference between the genuine numbers and the forged—the red ink is the bookmaker's—the type found on the prisoner would decidedly print 23 the same as on this forged ticket—anybody going into the enclosure with a forged ticket might come out and get a good ticket.
Cross-examined by MR. GEOGHEGAN. These tickets are not transferable, but we cannot prevent them being transferred.
THOMAS HOODLESS . I am a bookmaker and attend the metropolitan race meetings, and am known as doing so—I always bet in the small ring—the 2 s. enclosure at Croydon corresponds to the 2 s. 6 d. ring at other places—I don't remember seeing either of the prisoners before—these are some of my betting tickets—I have a clerk—at a race at Croydon people will come and ask me what I will lay, or I will call out the odds; if they are willing to take them they hand me their money and I hand a ticket and call out the bet to the clerk and the number of the ticket, and he enters them—after the race I pay
whoever produces the number—I don't recollect the identity of any particular person—I can't say that I have paid the same number more than once, I fancy I have—of these three tickets one has the proper number, on this the whole thing is erased, and on this are fresh lines—I should pay a ticket as soon as I saw it was all right; I should not pay particular notice.
Cross-examined by MR. GEOGHEGAN. These documents are called briefs—my brother is my clerk—losers always chuck their tickets away—I may have been done twice over—I am known at Kempton Park and Croydon—I should be safe if a cry of " Welsher " were raised—I could resist as to the validity of a bet without any fear—I think I have the moral courage to do it.
Cross-examined by MR. FRITH. I should only pay on the number to the man who produced the ticket—sometimes I think I have had to pay more than once—I make no charge against them.
JOHN LANGRISH ( Police Inspector, Criminal Investigation Department). In the course of my duty I have attended a good many racecourses including places like Croydon, where there are tickets for the enclosure—prior to 6th August complaints were made with regard to the tickets, and in consequence, on 6th August Bank Holiday, I went with Denny to the race meeting at Croydon and kept observation on people leaving for Croydon at the Cannon Street Railway Station—I first saw Cooper and Thomas Taylor together—I did not know them by sight—I saw them with a number of others whom I did not know and watched them—I followed them to Woodside Railway Station adjoining the course alone—Denny had left me at Cannon Street—they went on the course with two or three others, and I saw them go into the 2 s. enclosure and come out and go in again as the second race was run—the course was being cleared and I saw Cooper pass something to some one else on the course, and I gave the signal for their arrest—I did not know the man to whom the thing was passed or what the thing was—that was outside the enclosure, on the course—I saw him go in and out of the enclosure twice—I took Cooper with the assistance of Denny, who had joined me on the course, to some buildings adjoining the racecourse—I had given the order for both of them to be arrested—I searched Cooper first and found on him 10 of these Tom Hoodless's cards, these instruments, for printing, I believe, and 26 brass numerals, this piece of wood, two enclosure tickets and two pieces of paper, one containing black and the other red printing ink—the numerals and ink were in his inside coat pocket, and the bottle containing some fluid and the tickets were in the outside pocket—the numbers had been erased from nine of the 10 cards so that other numbers could be printed on them—when bookmakers on a racecourse give out a card to indicate the receipt of money for a bet, the man does not go back with the ticket if he does not win, he will throw it away—the brass numeral letters are not of the ordinary make, they have a number at each end; some of them are smaller at one end than the other—these 329 would correspond with Hoodless's ticket—the larger numbers corre-pond best with the enclosure tickets—I found marks on the oblong piece of wood, of numbers having been stamped on it, such as would be made by stamping a numeral on an intervening card—I have tried an experiment with the fluid in the bottle—it will rub off printing ink and enable it to be removed—I said to Cooper "How do you account for
possession of these type and tickets?"—he said "That is my business"—that was all—I searched Thomas Taylor and found on him a genuine enclosure ticket, some money, and two or three duffer rings in his pockets, useful to drop—he said nothing—before the prisoners were taken before the Magistrate I got the whole of the tickets taken at the enclosure gate—there are 48 corresponding numbers, duplicates—the 48 are differently executed to the others, being more roughly done; I can tell which are McCorquodale's and which are the forgeries—there is a curl on the 2—I went to McCorquodale's on several occasions, and from information, I took Brushwood in custody and charged him with conspiring to steal the tickets from McCorquodale's—I saw John Taylor at Southwark Police-court on 16th August—I had previously seen him at Clerkenwell Police-court; that was after I had arrested Cooper—Denny took John Taylor—Brushwood had made a communication to me on my first visit to McCorquodale's, and after that I saw John Taylor and had an interview with him—they went before the Magistrate on 7th August—they were remanded until Saturday, the 11th, Bench day—between those dates I saw John Taylor at Clerkenwell—I said "Have you ever been employed at McCorquodale's, the Armoury, Southwark?"—he said "I have"—I said "Have you ever printed tickets for the Croydon races?"—he said "I have"—I said "Can you describe to me the system by which the tickets are printed?"—I did not show him the tickets—he told me they passed through different departments, the last being the numbering—I asked him whether he knew Brushwood—he said "Yes, I know him"—I said "When did you see him last?"—he said "Saturday, the 3rd or 4th, I think; he called at my house, I think"—I said "When prior to that?"—he was not in custody at that time—he said "It was the Monday or Tuesday, I think, but I had had a little drink; I gee him as a rule two or three times a week"—I know Hoodless as a bookmaker—he was at Croydon races—I noticed when he was carrying on his business as bookmaker that Cooper and Thomas Taylor were very close to him on several occasions when he was calling out the numbers.
Cross-examined by MR. GEOGHEGAN. I swear I did not also see him close to other bookmakers—I said at the police-court in cross-examination "I saw him against several bookmakers, I saw nothing suspicious as regards that"—the bookmakers stand all together; they paid most attention to Hoodless—it was the Hoodless who was in Court on Saturday that they were near, I did not know at that time whether it was Tom or Harry—I go a good deal about the racecourses near London—I don't remember having seen Cooper at any racecourse before—I go in the interest of the Race Committee and the police to look after bad characters—I should look after welshers —I did not go with Sergeant Ham—I don't know sufficient about betting to know if it was a list of the acceptances for the St. Leger that was found on him; it was not for a cricket match at the Oval—I tried the stuff in the bottle, on a piece of paper stamped with ink, at the office, Scotland Yard—I rubbed it on and it obliterated the ink—it would very nearly do it on the 329 genuine card—I have not had it analysed—I have had an opinion about it—I do not know that part of its ingredients are chilies—I have never heard the joke about wetting a wineglass with chilies—I did not take Cooper into custody until the second race was run—I kept my eyes on him and his companions the whole time—Denny and four other officers were with me when I saw Cooper pass something
to a man unknown—they were clearing the course, and they could not take the other man, he was lost—I sent off a uniform officer, I don't know his number or name—I said "Look after that man"—he belongs to the P division—I cannot say if he is a sergeant, the rush was so great—I believe the two enclosure tickets found on Cooper were genuine, I did not print them—at Croydon I charged Cooper and the other two prisoners with loitering with intent to commit felony, and the Magistrate discharged them—after that I brought them up at the Southwark Police-court—I received my instructions from Messrs. Wontner, the solicitors to the Race Committee—the night he was taken I made inquiries about Cooper—I said at the police-court "I have not seen Cooper at other race meetings; I find he is a tool-cutter, living at 25, St. James's Walk, Clerkenwell"—he works at the shop of a telescope-maker—I found several tools in his shop—when I asked him to account for the possession of this type he said "That is my business."
Cross-examined by MR. FRITH. Nothing relative to this charge was found on Thomas or John Taylor—I saw Thomas Taylor in London at the railway station with half a dozen to a dozen racing men going down, and afterwards with Cooper at Croydon—John Taylor I never saw before in my life—when I commenced this series of questions he was not in custody, I did not intend to take him then; Brushwood had not given the information then—I was investigating the matter, and thought it a proper act—I did not argue with him.
By MR. GEOGHEGAN. Tickets very similar to this, only on paper are used as advertisements and thrown about—I thinly the ordinary printer makes the betting card design—I cannot say if a special class make it.
Re-examined. The last time the prisoners were before the Bench at Croydon it was suggested by Mr. Wontner that another charge would be made at Southwark of stealing tickets; it was suggested that Mr. Boardman should let them be on bail, and Mr. Wontner suggested that the proper course would be to discharge them, and I was to take them into custody immediately on the other charge—I did not know at that time that 70 tickets had been stolen, nor at the time I had the conversation with John Taylor, and so I asked him the same as I should any other person about the matter—this paper is the acceptances fox the city and Suburban, 18th April.
WALTER DENNY ( Detective Sergeant, Criminal Investigation Department). I was instructed to act with Langrish with regard to the Croydon race meeting, on 6th August, and had had information about tickets being dealt with improperly—I was with Langrish on Bank Holiday morning at the railway station, watching the people depart for the races—I saw Cooper and Thomas Taylor at Cannon Street Railway Station—I did not see them get into the train, I went away and followed afterwards, and rejoined Langrish, who had gone by that train, outside the racecourse—we went on the course together, and kept observation on these men, and saw them going about on the course and into the 2 s. enclosure; we watched them there—I saw Tom Hoodless betting in the 2 s. enclosure—when bookmakers are betting they call out the numbers loud enough for anybody to hear—I saw both the men standing near Hoodless for some time while he was betting—I was with Langrish when he gave the order for them to be taken in custody—I heard him give his evidence at the
police-court with regard to what took place—before they were arrested they were mixing with the crowd, and I saw him pass his hand before another man—I confirm what Langrish said as to the arrest.
Cross-examined by MR. GEOGHEGAN. I saw Cooper go on the course—when he was not in the 2 s. enclosure he was near Hoodless—he was nearer Hoodless than any other bookmaker—the bookmakers do not all stand together—Hoodless was a little way off the other bookmakers—I had gone into the 2 s. enclosure before the first race—I had seen Cooper standing near Hoodless before the first race—Hoodless made bets on the first and second races—I did not see Cooper go up with one of these duffing tickets after the first or second race—when Cooper passed something to the man, Langrish, Ward, and Visard were my companions, Langrish and I close together, and the others a little behind—nobody followed after that other man, there was such a crowd—I won't swear Langrish did not—Langrish was a little in front of me—I could not very well say I saw one of our body separate himself and go after the man—several constables of the P division were with us—Inspector Blackwell was with Langrish and me when we took Cooper, or just before—I don't know if he was in uniform or plain clothes—I am not in the habit of going with Langrish to race meetings—I have been to Croydon and Kempton before—I have never seen Cooper before.
COOPER received a good character— GUILTY of conspiracy and forgery. — Eighteen Months' Hard Labour. JOHN TAYLOR and THOMAS TAYLOR — GUILTY of conspiracy only. JOHN TAYLOR — Nine Months' Hard Labour. THOMAS TAYLOR — Twelve Months' Hurt Labour.
[trialAccount t18830910-886]
886. JOHN HICKSON, Embezzling 55 l. 7 s. 4 1/2 d., the moseys of John Pound, his master.
MESSRS. THORNE COLE and BURNIE Prosecuted; MR. WILDEY WRIGHT Defended.
JOHN POUND . I live at Grove Park, Lee, and am proprietor and licensed owner of the Baring Hall Hotel there—the prisoner and his wife came into my service in 1881 under this agreement ( produced), by which the prisoner would have the management of the public-house—they went on in my service till 24th June, when the stock was taken—on 27th July he gave me notice to leave—on 26th August I instructed my clerk to take stock again, and in consequence of what he said a warrant was applied for—I carried out my part of the agreement—the prisoner never complained to me after 24th June of being short of stock.
Cross-examined. I have considerable experience in the trade—the prisoner was of good character enough for me to engage him—I left everything financially and as to practical details in connection with the house to Basnett, my managing clerk—I saw the prisoner nearly every day—he did not complain to me that could not get stock in sufficiently quickly, or that it was an injury to the business—it came to my knowledge—Basnett told me of the deficiency, and I undertook the prosecution.
WILLIAM BASNETT . I am the prosecutor's clerk—I am accustomed to take stock at this hotel, at which the prisoner was manager—it is in a new neighbourhood, and does not do a large business, I should think on an average about 18 l. to 20 l. per week—a man and his wife could attend to it without any difficulty—I took stock on 24th June—I have got the book ( produced) in which the value of the stock is set forth—I went ever
it with the prisoner on that occasion, and made the entries from his instructions—the exact figures of the quantity were given by him in my presence—I did not put them down in this book, but in this office book—they were copied from the office book into the stock book by my clerk, and taken to the prisoner the following day—the stock book and the items were gone through carefully—I was present on both occasions—there was then 108 l. 6 s. 10 d. worth of stock—on the following afternoon, Sunday, I filled in this book and took it to him—I said, "Everything you receive in the hotel for the future write down in this stock book"—from 24th June to 26th August entries are made in his writing in the stock and takings books—on 26th August I took stock again with him, between 28th June and 26th August he received 298 l. 14 s., making with the 108 l. 6 s. 10 d. a total of 407 l. 0 s. 10 d.—the total amount paid to me was 247 l. 18 s. 5 d., so that there ought to have been stock on the premises of the value of 159 l. 2 s. 5 d.—the quantify of the stock was measured carefully and given by himself in the presence of the incoming manager and the clerk at the office—he had then 103 l. 14 s. 7 1/2 d., leaving a deficiency of stock of 55 l. 7 s. 9 1/2 d.—I received from him the moneys taken, supposed to be the exact takings on behalf of the prosecutor—when we had completed the stocktaking I said, "Is that all you have got?"—he said "Yes"—I saw him on the Sunday evening; he had then left the premises—I said, "There is something rascally wrong about this; there is 55 l. 7 s. 9 1/2 d. short—he said, "Yes, I know it"—I said, "Can't you rake up more money to lessen this deficit before I have to report the same?"—he said, "No, I haven't got any money"—I said, "Well, do you think there is any mistake? because if you do, for my own satisfaction as well as your own I would prefer going through the stock again"—he said, "No, I don't think so; the stock is all right, you can't make any more of it"—I said, "I am not satisfied"—I saw he was excited—I said, "Very well, meet me here to-morrow morning, say at 9.30, and we will go through the whole thing again to see if we can make any difference in it"—I attended there on the Monday morning; he failed to turn up, and I went to where he was temporarily living, about 300 yards from the hotel—I did not see him—I left a message that I should call again in the evening—when I went there I found he had gone by the 12 o'clock train—on Tuesday evening, the 28th, he came to my house—I said, "Have you found out anything about this stock?"—he said "No"—I said, "What is to be done?"—he said, "I don't know"—I said, "Haven't you got any money?"—he said "No"—I said, " Then it is not a fact that when you took the train from Grove Park Station at midday on Saturday that in getting into a third-class railway carriage you stumbled and dropped your bag which you had in your hand, the contents rolling on the carriage floor, consisting of gold and silver?" —he said, "What shall I do?"—I said, "If you will take my advice, come to the office to-morrow morning as early as you can, say from 10 to 10.30, and I will take you in front of Mr. Pound with a view to see if he can come to any arrangement"—on the Sunday at the hotel he had said, "You know as well as I can that my wife has been robbing me, in fact it has nearly driven me mad"—on Wednesday morning he came to the office—I said to him, "You know this is a bad job altogether; it seems as if over a pound a day has been going away; have you got any offer to make?"—he said, "Yes, I want you to ask Mr. Pound if he will take 15 l. now, which I have at home, and allow me a little time to pay the difference"—
I submitted it to Mr. Pound; he would not entertain it—I came back and told the prisoner so, and that a warrant was out for his apprehension—since the new manager came in there is an increase of over 1 l. a day.
Cross-examined. I have the books that show that; they are in my writing—that is the only book I have here which shows it—the takings were larger in corresponding weeks in September last year than this year—the weeks vary from time to time—I nave been with Mr. Pound five years, and have been managing clerk to this house since its opening in October, 1881; previous to that I had had no practical experience of public-houses—I was a sergeant major in the army, and had a good deal to do with the stock in the canteens and officers' mess—I Know the ordinary way of taking stock—we hadn't a proper gauging-stick for the spirits; we used a deal lath; it answered the purpose—you could get it within a quarter of a pint—the manager after a little calculation would give the quantities—I put it in the book, and worked it out afterwards—on 24th June I put down the quantities as called out and decided on—in this book all these are "quarts," because they were decanted—the original entries I made were in pencil; they have been entered in ink in his presence since—this ( produced) is the rough book—ink is written over the pencil in every entry in this book—every item in this book was prepared previous to going to the hotel, except the figures—the ink was an exact fac-simile of the pencil, only the figures were written over—to the best of my belief every one of the figures were originally in pencil—on 26th August all the figures were not previously in pencil; they were copied from this book—it was not done in the same way in August as in June—I have repeated the conversations from memory; I have not been tutored in any way—I did not tell the prisoner that if he would only put the matter right I would be his friend, or that if he would find 15 l. and pay the rest by instalments I would get Mr. Pound to put the rest right—I suggested that he should rake up some money to reduce the deficiency, and if he had I would have taken it—the figures 108 l. 6 s. 10 d. were the result of my own working out—I did not take stock at all between 24th June and 26th August—books were kept at the hotel showing a proper retail account of wines, spirits, ales, mineral waters, and cigars—I several times got goods of the prisoner and paid for them—the only time no money passed was when I joined a Christmas club there—I did not get the spirits which I said I would take instead of the 1 l. 12 s. 8 d. due to me, and so I wrote and told him to send me certain things, and no money passed—I don't know whether his club accounts were right—I know his wife took to drinking, and had as much access to the till as he had—I believe her drinking bouts lasted for a long time—he repeatedly told me they were a great source of trouble to him—I have never heard of one spot or blemish on his character.
Re-examined. There were only a few bottles and casks broken into, and it was only in those cases we used the gauge—I took the figures from the defendant himself.
WILLIAM ANDREW BASNETT . I am the last witness's son, and clerk to Mr. Pound—I accompanied my father on Sunday, 26th August—I assisted at the stocktaking; the prisoner was present—I heard what was said at the interview on Tuesday; what my father has said about it is correct.
JOHN SNELGROVE ( Policeman). I am stationed at Bromley—on 31st August
I arrested the prisoner on a warrant at his lodgings about 600 yards from the hotel—I charged him—he said "Let me have a look at it"—he was lying in bed—I did so—he said "I suppose I must go," and got up and dressed, and I took him to Lee Station, where he was charged.
MR. WRIGHT submitted that evidence of a general deficiency would not support a charge of embezzlement, and that there was no evidence of the embezzlement of any specific sum. He cited Reg. v. Lloyd Jones (8 carrington and Payne, 288), Reg. v. Chapman (Carrington and Kirwan, 119), Reg. v. Wolstenholms (11 Cox's Criminal Cases, p. 313). MR. BURNIE submitted that the amount stated in the indictment was not material if any sum was shown to be embezzled. He cited Rex v. Grove (2 Russell on Crimes, 459), Reg. v. King (12 Cox's Criminal Cases, p. 73), and Reg. v. Balls (12 Cox, p. 96). The COMMON SERJEANT directed the Jury to find a verdict of
NOT GUILTY .

CORRECTION.
In the case of Arthur Wright and others, page 439, the ruling of the COMMON SERJEANT should read as follows: That it was not necessary that the bank books should be produced, and that the copy of the entry was admissible under the 5th Section of the Bankers" Books Evidence Act of 1879 (42 Vic. c. 11), which enabled proof of the examination of the copy with the original entry to be given orally by the person who made the examination.
